Thus~$\rho$ is closure decreasing,
and Theorem~\ref{thm:termination} yields termination of $\rho$.
\end{example}\par Finally, we consider a hypergraph example with unrestricted matching. Our method can prove relative termination of the first rule, but termination of the second rule does not seem to be provable with our method.

\end{center}over the arithmetic semiring by Theorem~\ref{thm:termination}.
Here the `$+$' hyperedge has weight $2$.
\par However, $\tau$ is an interesting rule for which we could not find a termination proof using weighted type graphs over the arctic, tropical or arithmetic semiring.
First, note that there exists an epimorphism $e:R\twoheadrightarrow L$ with $l=e\circ r$.
In such situations, no weighted type graph over the arithmetic semiring can make the rule strictly decreasing.
Second, it seems that the arctic and tropical semiring are also not applicable here. Intuitively, one might try to use the tropical semiring since the additional freedom in mapping $R$ could lead to a lower weight.
However, for this rule, there are morphisms $h_{1},h_{2}:L\to R$ where $h_{1}$ maps $L$ onto the left component of $R$, and $h_{2}$ maps $L$ onto the right component of $R$.
So, for any mapping $t:R\to T$, either $t\circ h_{1}$ or $t\circ h_{2}$ will be at least as `light'.
\par In~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}]}, it is argued that this rule is terminating since the sum of the squares of in-degrees is decreasing in each step. This argument can be automated, for instance, using the technique proposed in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2024termination}{}{}, Example~5.9]}: see Section~\ref{discussion:termination:of:graph:transformation} for a related discussion.
\end{example}\par\samepage Example~\ref{ex:limitations} illustrates a limitation of our technique:
\begin{open}We conjecture that our technique cannot be applied for rules $\rho:L\mathrel{{\mathop{\leftarrow}\limits}}K\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}R$ for which there exists an epimorphism $e:R\twoheadrightarrow L$ with $l=e\circ r$.
Can the technique be enhanced to deal with such rules?
\end{open}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Implementation and Benchmarks}
\par We have implemented our techniques in {graphTT-wtg} (short for {graph} {T}ermination {T}ool -- {w}eighted {t}ype {g}raphs) for automatically proving
termination of graph transformation.
The tool is written in Scala and employs Z3~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{z3}{}{}]} to solve the constraint systems.\par The tool allows to specify the underlying category using {{copresheaves}}, i.e., functors $F:{\textbf{I}}\to{\textbf{Set}}$, which are equivalent to {{unary algebras}}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{lowe1993algebraic}{}{}]} and $\mathcal{C}$-sets~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{julia}{}{}]}.
The grammar for specifying the index category ${\textbf{I}}$ is
\@@amsalign
where {Name} is the name of an object in ${\textbf{I}}$, and the `arguments' are the targets of outgoing arrows. Optionally, an object can be equipped with a set of labels which corresponds to a slice category construction.
{graphTT-wtg} can thereby be applied to the multigraph examples~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bruggink2014,bruggink2015}{}{}]}, and the hypergraph examples~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}]}.
\par Moreover, the tool supports what could be called `partially simple' presheaves in which some of the element sets are simple (the elements are uniquely determined by their `argument values'). To this end, an object declaration can be followed by `!'.
For instance
\@@amsalign
describes presheaves having a set of nodes $V$,
binary edges,
ternary hyperedges `plus' between nodes of $V$,
and unary hyperedges `flag' labelled with labels from $\{a,b\}$.
The binary edges allow for multiple parallel edges. The ternary `plus' is declared as simple, meaning that there cannot be two `plus' edges between the same tuple of nodes (the order matters).

\@add@centering\end{table}\par{graphTT-wtg} supports a simple strategy language of the form
\@@amsalign
This enables the user to specify what technique is applied in what order and for how long.
Each strategy can transform the system by removing rules.
A strategy is considered successful if at least one rule has been removed.
For the basis strategies, that is, arctic, tropical or arithmetic weighted type graphs, `size' specifies the size of the type graph, `bits' the number of bits for encoding the semiring elements, and `timeout' is the the maximum number of seconds to try this technique.
\par Table~\ref{tabular:benchmarks} shows benchmarks for running {graphTT-wtg} on the examples from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bruggink2014,bruggink2015,plump1995on,plump2018modular}{}{}]} under different combinations of the arctic, tropical and arithmetic semiring.
The techniques proposed in the current paper successfully establish termination of all examples, with the exception of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}, Example 6]}.
See Example~\ref{ex:limitations} for a discussion about this system.
\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Related Work}
\par The approaches~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bruggink2014,bruggink2015}{}{}]} by Bruggink et al.\ are the most relevant to our paper. Both employ weighted type graphs for multigraphs to prove termination of graph transformation.
We significantly strengthen the weighted type graphs technique for rewriting with monic matching and we generalize the techniques to arbitrary categories and different variants of DPO.
\par Plump has proposed two systematic termination criteria~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump1995on,plump2018modular}{}{}]} for hypergraph rewriting with DPO.
The approach in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump1995on}{}{}]} is based on the concept of forward closures.
The paper~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}]} introduces a technique for modular termination proofs based on sequential critical pairs. The modular approach can often simplify the termination arguments significantly. However, the smaller decomposed systems still require termination arguments.
Therefore, our techniques and the approach from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}]} complement each other perfectly.
Our technique can handle all examples from the papers~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump1995on,plump2018modular}{}{}]}, except for one (see Example~\ref{ex:limitations} for discussion of this system).
\par In~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2024termination}{}{}]}, we have proposed a termination approach based on weighted element counting.
Like the method presented in this paper, the method~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2024termination}{}{}]} is defined for general categories (more specifically, rm-adhesive quasitoposes), but for PBPO${}^{+}${}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2023quasitoposes,overbeek2024phdthesis}{}{}]} instead of DPO. PBPO${}^{+}${} subsumes DPO in the quasitopos setting~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2023quasitoposes}{}{}, Theorem 72]} if both the left-hand morphism $l$ of DPO rules and matches $m$ are required to be regular monic.
The weighted element counting approach defines the weight of an object $G$ by means of counting weighted elements of the form $t:T\to G$, meaning it is roughly dual to the approach presented in this paper.
\par An example that the weighted type graph approach can prove, but the weighted element counting approach cannot prove, is Example~\ref{example:tree:counter}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bruggink2015}{}{}, Example 6]}, which requires measuring a global property rather than a local one.
Conversely, Example~\ref{ex:limitations}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump2018modular}{}{}, Example 6]} is an example that seems unprovable using weighted type graphs, while the element counting approach can prove it rather straightforwardly~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2024termination}{}{}, Example~5.9]}. So it appears the two methods may be complementary even in settings where they are both defined.
\par Levendovszky et al.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{injective:2007}{}{}]} propose a criterion for termination of DPO rules with respect to injective matching. Roughly speaking, they show that a system is terminating if the size of repeated sequential compositions of rules tends to infinity. It remains to be seen if this criterion can be fruitfully automated.
\par Bottoni et al.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{hlr2005termination}{}{}]} introduce a framework for proving termination of high-level replacement units (these are systems with external control expressions). They show that their framework can be used for node and edge counting arguments which are subsumed by weighted type graphs (see further~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bruggink2015}{}{}]}).
\par There are also various techniques that generalize TRS methods to term graphs~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{plump1999term,plump97simplification,moser2016kruskal}{}{}]} and drags~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{dershowitz2018graph}{}{}]}.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Future Work}
\par There are several interesting directions for future research:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Can the weighted type graphs technique be extended to other algebraic graph transformation frameworks, such as SPO~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{lowe1993algebraic}{}{}]}, SqPO~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{corradini2006sesqui}{}{}]}, PBPO~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{corradini2019pbpo}{}{}]}, AGREE~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{corradini2020algebraic}{}{}]} and PBPO${}^{+}${}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2023quasitoposes}{}{}]}?
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Can the technique be enhanced to deal with examples like Example~\ref{ex:limitations}?
(See also Open Question~\ref{open:epi}.)
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Can the technique be extended to deal with negative application conditions~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{dpo:negative}{}{}]}?
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Are there semirings other than arctic, tropical and arithmetic that can be fruitfully employed for termination proofs with the weighted type graph technique?
\end{enumerate}Concerning~\eqref{future:frameworks}, an extension to PBPO${}^{+}${} would be particularly interesting.
In~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{overbeek2023quasitoposes}{}{}]}, it has been shown that, in the setting of quasitoposes, every rule of the other formalisms can be straightforwardly encoded as a PBPO${}^{+}${} rule that generates the same rewrite relation.
However, our proofs (Lemma~\ref{lem:one:side}) crucially depend on a property specific to pushouts (Lemma~\ref{lem:bijection}). It will therefore be challenging to lift the reasoning to the pullbacks in PBPO${}^{+}${} without (heavily) restricting the framework (to those pullbacks that are pushouts).
